========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... no, I don't think it physically changes. I mean, I know that just looking at my phone screen or checking a mirror doesn't actually alter my face.

Um, especially before my work meetings or if I'm supposed to go out somewhere. It's more that... I just couldn't handle it. If I don't check, the anxiety just builds up until I can't focus on anything else. I feel like everyone is staring at that side of my face and judging me. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, I kind of just said this, but no, not terribly wrong like an emergency. It's really just about the anxiety. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because... I just can't stop focusing on my face. Specifically, the left side of it. It looks really uneven and puffy to me. \n\nI have to be on video calls a lot for work, and I see it there constantly, and in photos from my salsa classes. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything at all when I point it out, but... I do. It's getting to the point where it's really starting to mess with my work and my social life, so I figured I needed to come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of how you feel about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, especially before my work meetings or if I'm supposed to go out somewhere. I just keep opening the camera preview on my screen or my phone. \n\nI'll sit there changing the angle, adjusting the lighting, and... just fixing my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it or make it look normal. I end up retaking pictures over and over to check it, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I just get stuck doing it until I'm either running late, or... honestly, sometimes I just get too drained from the whole process to even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just my face. I mean, I worry about normal things like work deadlines or, you know, paying bills, but nothing like the way I obsess over my face. That's the only thing that gets me stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face led to you avoiding any specific social interactions or work-related activities because of how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. Um, at work, I've actually joined some of my video calls late because I just couldn't get past how I looked on the camera preview. I've even missed a few ticket deadlines because I was so distracted by it. My supervisor actually brought it up in my last performance review, which was really embarrassing. \n\nAnd outside of work, yeah, I've skipped some salsa nights and other community events. Especially if I know people are going to be taking photos. I just can't deal with the anxiety of seeing myself in them later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to enjoy things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. It even came up in my performance review with my supervisor, which was tough to hear. \n\nAnd as far as things I enjoy... yeah. I used to love going to salsa nights and different community events, but I've been skipping them. If I know there's a chance people will be taking pictures, I just don't go. It's just not worth the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals to try and make yourself feel better about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I have a meeting or if I'm trying to go out, I get stuck in this loop. I'll open the camera preview on my phone or computer and just keep adjusting the angle and the lighting. I try to fix my hair so it covers that side of my face better. \n\nI end up taking picture after picture, just trying to see if it looks okay, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I do it over and over until I end up being late, or sometimes I just get so drained from the whole process that I don't even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say something *bad* would happen, like a disaster or anything. It's more that... I just couldn't handle it. If I don't check, the anxiety just builds up until I can't focus on anything else. I feel like everyone is staring at that side of my face and judging me. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, I kind of just said this, but no, not terribly wrong like an emergency. It's really just about the anxiety. If I don't check it or try to fix it, I just feel so exposed and panicked that I can't function. That's the only thing that goes wrong, just my own reaction to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would actually look different to others if you didn't perform these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I don't think it physically changes. I mean, I know that just looking at my phone screen or checking a mirror doesn't actually alter my face. \n\nIt's more that... if I don't check, I just don't know exactly how bad it looks in that specific lighting or angle. I guess adjusting my hair changes how much of it people can see, so maybe that part looks different to them. But the actual left side of my face? No, I know that stays the same whether I perform those checks or not. I just... I need to see it so I know what I'm dealing with before I let anyone else see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about your face would decrease if you couldn't check or fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually been in a situation where I couldn't check it at all for a long period of time. But... based on how I feel when I just try to delay checking, I don't think my anxiety would decrease. I think it would probably just stay really high, because I'd just be sitting there obsessing over how bad it might look.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Um, I think it's a little bit of both. To me, it just looks inherently wrong. I mean, it doesn't match the other side, so it feels unacceptable to me personally when I look at it.

Um, especially before my work meetings or if I'm supposed to go out somewhere. I just keep opening the camera preview on my screen or my phone. \n\nI'll sit there changing the angle, adjusting the lighting, and... just fixing my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it or make it look normal. I end up retaking pictures over and over to check it, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I just get stuck doing it until I'm either running late, or... honestly, sometimes I just get too drained from the whole process to even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just my face. I mean, I worry about normal things like work deadlines or, you know, paying bills, but nothing like the way I obsess over my face. That's the only thing that gets me stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face led to you avoiding any specific social interactions or work-related activities because of how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. Um, at work, I've actually joined some of my video calls late because I just couldn't get past how I looked on the camera preview. I've even missed a few ticket deadlines because I was so distracted by it. My supervisor actually brought it up in my last performance review, which was really embarrassing. \n\nAnd outside of work, yeah, I've skipped some salsa nights and other community events. Especially if I know people are going to be taking photos. I just can't deal with the anxiety of seeing myself in them later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to enjoy things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Like I mentioned, it's getting in the way of my job. I've missed those ticket deadlines and been late to video calls because I'm just stuck staring at how I look on the screen. It even came up in my performance review with my supervisor, which was tough to hear. \n\nAnd as far as things I enjoy... yeah. I used to love going to salsa nights and different community events, but I've been skipping them. If I know there's a chance people will be taking pictures, I just don't go. It's just not worth the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals to try and make yourself feel better about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I have a meeting or if I'm trying to go out, I get stuck in this loop. I'll open the camera preview on my phone or computer and just keep adjusting the angle and the lighting. I try to fix my hair so it covers that side of my face better. \n\nI end up taking picture after picture, just trying to see if it looks okay, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I do it over and over until I end up being late, or sometimes I just get so drained from the whole process that I don't even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say something *bad* would happen, like a disaster or anything. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... I think it's mostly about trying to achieve a look where it doesn't seem so uneven. Like, before my work meetings or if I'm going out, I'll just keep opening my camera preview.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because... I just can't stop focusing on my face. Specifically, the left side of it. It looks really uneven and puffy to me. \n\nI have to be on video calls a lot for work, and I see it there constantly, and in photos from my salsa classes. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything at all when I point it out, but... I do. It's getting to the point where it's really starting to mess with my work and my social life, so I figured I needed to come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of how you feel about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, especially before my work meetings or if I'm supposed to go out somewhere. I just keep opening the camera preview on my screen or my phone. \n\nI'll sit there changing the angle, adjusting the lighting, and... just fixing my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it or make it look normal. I end up retaking pictures over and over to check it, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I just get stuck doing it until I'm either running late, or... honestly, sometimes I just get too drained from the whole process to even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just my face. I mean, I worry about normal things like work deadlines or, you know, paying bills, but nothing like the way I obsess over my face. That's the only thing that gets me stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face led to you avoiding any specific social interactions or work-related activities because of how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. Um, at work, I've actually joined some of my video calls late because I just couldn't get past how I looked on the camera preview. I've even missed a few ticket deadlines because I was so distracted by it. My supervisor actually brought it up in my last performance review, which was really embarrassing. \n\nAnd outside of work, yeah, I've skipped some salsa nights and other community events. Especially if I know people are going to be taking photos. I just can't deal with the anxiety of seeing myself in them later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to enjoy things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Like I mentioned, it's getting in the way of my job. I've missed those ticket deadlines and been late to video calls because I'm just stuck staring at how I look on the screen. It even came up in my performance review with my supervisor, which was tough to hear. \n\nAnd as far as things I enjoy... yeah. I used to love going to salsa nights and different community events, but I've been skipping them. If I know there's a chance people will be taking pictures, I just don't go. It's just not worth the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals to try and make yourself feel better about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I have a meeting or if I'm trying to go out, I get stuck in this loop. I'll open the camera preview on my phone or computer and just keep adjusting the angle and the lighting. I try to fix my hair so it covers that side of my face better. \n\nI end up taking picture after picture, just trying to see if it looks okay, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I do it over and over until I end up being late, or sometimes I just get so drained from the whole process that I don't even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say something *bad* would happen, like a disaster or anything. It's more that... I just couldn't handle it. If I don't check, the anxiety just builds up until I can't focus on anything else. I feel like everyone is staring at that side of my face and judging me. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, I kind of just said this, but no, not terribly wrong like an emergency. It's really just about the anxiety. If I don't check it or try to fix it, I just feel so exposed and panicked that I can't function. That's the only thing that goes wrong, just my own reaction to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would actually look different to others if you didn't perform these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I don't think it physically changes. I mean, I know that just looking at my phone screen or checking a mirror doesn't actually alter my face. \n\nIt's more that... if I don't check, I just don't know exactly how bad it looks in that specific lighting or angle. I guess adjusting my hair changes how much of it people can see, so maybe that part looks different to them. But the actual left side of my face? No, I know that stays the same whether I perform those checks or not. I just... I need to see it so I know what I'm dealing with before I let anyone else see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about your face would decrease if you couldn't check or fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually been in a situation where I couldn't check it at all for a long period of time. But... based on how I feel when I just try to delay checking, I don't think my anxiety would decrease. I think it would probably just stay really high, because I'd just be sitting there obsessing over how bad it might look.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way or be perfectly symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. I feel like it needs to be even. Honestly, that's why I finally came in to see someone. I just... I can't stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks. \n\nIt stands out so much to me on video calls for work, or when I see photos of myself from salsa dancing. I just want it to look symmetrical so I can feel comfortable. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything, but I can't stop seeing it, and it's really starting to mess with my job and my social life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something inherently 'wrong' or 'unacceptable' about the left side of your face, or is it more about fearing how others might react to it?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's a little bit of both. To me, it just looks inherently wrong. I mean, it doesn't match the other side, so it feels unacceptable to me personally when I look at it. \n\nBut... I also definitely worry about how other people are reacting to it. Even though my friends or coworkers say they don't notice anything, I'm always afraid they're just being polite and that they actually think it looks strange. So I guess I feel like it's wrong, and because of that, I'm afraid of what they're thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive behaviors are more about achieving a certain look or preventing something from happening because of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly about trying to achieve a look where it doesn't seem so uneven. Like, before my work meetings or if I'm going out, I'll just keep opening my camera preview. I try changing the angle, adjusting the lighting... I'll fix my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it. \n\nI end up retaking pictures over and over, and asking my friends if I look strange. I just keep doing it until I'm either late or honestly just too drained to even go out. So I guess I'm just trying to fix how it looks before people see me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything, but I can't stop seeing it, and it's really starting to mess with my job and my social life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something inherently 'wrong' or 'unacceptable' about the left side of your face, or is it more about fearing how others might react to it?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's a little bit of both. To me, it just looks inherently wrong. I mean, it doesn't match the other side, so it feels unacceptable to me personally when I look at it. \n\nBut... I also definitely worry about how other people are reacting to it. Even though my friends or coworkers say they don't notice anything, I'm always afraid they're just being polite and that they actually think it looks strange. So I guess I feel like it's wrong, and because of that, I'm afraid of what they're thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive behaviors are more about achieving a certain look or preventing something from happening because of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly about trying to achieve a look where it doesn't seem so uneven. Like, before my work meetings or if I'm going out, I'll just keep opening my camera preview. I try changing the angle, adjusting the lighting... I'll fix my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it. \n\nI end up retaking pictures over and over, and asking my friends if I look strange. I just keep doing it until I'm either late or honestly just too drained to even go out. I don't know, I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... I'm not totally sure. Most of the time when I'm checking it, it's because I'm getting ready for a meeting or getting ready to go out.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because... I just can't stop focusing on my face. Specifically, the left side of it. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything at all when I point it out, but... I do. It's getting to the point where it's really starting to mess with my work and my social life, so I figured I needed to come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of how you feel about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, especially before my work meetings or if I'm supposed to go out somewhere. I just keep opening the camera preview on my screen or my phone. \n\nI'll sit there changing the angle, adjusting the lighting, and... just fixing my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it or make it look normal. I end up retaking pictures over and over to check it, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I just get stuck doing it until I'm either running late, or... honestly, sometimes I just get too drained from the whole process to even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just my face. I mean, I worry about normal things like work deadlines or, you know, paying bills, but nothing like the way I obsess over my face. That's the only thing that gets me stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face led to you avoiding any specific social interactions or work-related activities because of how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. Um, at work, I've actually joined some of my video calls late because I just couldn't get past how I looked on the camera preview. I've even missed a few ticket deadlines because I was so distracted by it. My supervisor actually brought it up in my last performance review, which was really embarrassing. \n\nAnd outside of work, yeah, I've skipped some salsa nights and other community events. Especially if I know people are going to be taking photos. I just can't deal with the anxiety of seeing myself in them later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to enjoy things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Like I mentioned, it's getting in the way of my job. I've missed those ticket deadlines and been late to video calls because I'm just stuck staring at how I look on the screen. It even came up in my performance review with my supervisor, which was tough to hear. \n\nAnd as far as things I enjoy... yeah. I used to love going to salsa nights and different community events, but I've been skipping them. If I know there's a chance people will be taking pictures, I just don't go. It's just not worth the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals to try and make yourself feel better about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I have a meeting or if I'm trying to go out, I get stuck in this loop. I'll open the camera preview on my phone or computer and just keep adjusting the angle and the lighting. I try to fix my hair so it covers that side of my face better. \n\nI end up taking picture after picture, just trying to see if it looks okay, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I do it over and over until I end up being late, or sometimes I just get so drained from the whole process that I don't even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say something *bad* would happen, like a disaster or anything. It's more that... I just couldn't handle it. If I don't check, the anxiety just builds up until I can't focus on anything else. I feel like everyone is staring at that side of my face and judging me. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... It looks really uneven and puffy to me. \n\nI have to be on video calls a lot for work, and I see it there constantly, and in photos from my salsa classes. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything at all when I point it out, but... I do. It's getting to the point where it's really starting to mess with my work and my social life, so I figured I needed to come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of how you feel about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, especially before my work meetings or if I'm supposed to go out somewhere. I just keep opening the camera preview on my screen or my phone. \n\nI'll sit there changing the angle, adjusting the lighting, and... just fixing my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it or make it look normal. I just get stuck doing it until I'm either running late, or... honestly, sometimes I just get too drained from the whole process to even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just my face. I mean, I worry about normal things like work deadlines or, you know, paying bills, but nothing like the way I obsess over my face. That's the only thing that gets me stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face led to you avoiding any specific social interactions or work-related activities because of how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. Um, at work, I've actually joined some of my video calls late because I just couldn't get past how I looked on the camera preview. I've even missed a few ticket deadlines because I was so distracted by it. My supervisor actually brought it up in my last performance review, which was really embarrassing. \n\nAnd outside of work, yeah, I've skipped some salsa nights and other community events. Especially if I know people are going to be taking photos. I just can't deal with the anxiety of seeing myself in them later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to enjoy things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Like I mentioned, it's getting in the way of my job. I've missed those ticket deadlines and been late to video calls because I'm just stuck staring at how I look on the screen. It even came up in my performance review with my supervisor, which was tough to hear. \n\nAnd as far as things I enjoy... yeah. I used to love going to salsa nights and different community events, but I've been skipping them. If I know there's a chance people will be taking pictures, I just don't go. It's just not worth the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals to try and make yourself feel better about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I have a meeting or if I'm trying to go out, I get stuck in this loop. I'll open the camera preview on my phone or computer and just keep adjusting the angle and the lighting. I try to fix my hair so it covers that side of my face better. \n\nI end up taking picture after picture, just trying to see if it looks okay, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I do it over and over until I end up being late, or sometimes I just get so drained from the whole process that I don't even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say something *bad* would happen, like a disaster or anything. It's more that... I just couldn't handle it. If I don't check, the anxiety just builds up until I can't focus on anything else. I feel like everyone is staring at that side of my face and judging me. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... I've never actually been in a situation where I couldn't check it at all for a long period of time. I think it would probably just stay really high, because I'd just be sitting there obsessing over how bad it might look.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way or be perfectly symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. I just keep doing it until I'm either late or honestly just too drained to even go out. So I guess I'm just trying to fix how it looks before people see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and fix your face if you were completely alone and nobody was going to see you?\n\nPatient: I don't know, I've never really thought about it like that. So, the anxiety wouldn't completely go away, because I'm still the one seeing it and feeling like it's wrong. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because... I just can't stop focusing on my face. Specifically, the left side of it. It looks really uneven and puffy to me. \n\nI have to be on video calls a lot for work, and I see it there constantly, and in photos from my salsa classes. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything at all when I point it out, but... I do. It's getting to the point where it's really starting to mess with my work and my social life, so I figured I needed to come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of how you feel about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, especially before my work meetings or if I'm supposed to go out somewhere. I just keep opening the camera preview on my screen or my phone. \n\nI'll sit there changing the angle, adjusting the lighting, and... just fixing my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it or make it look normal. I end up retaking pictures over and over to check it, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I just get stuck doing it until I'm either running late, or... honestly, sometimes I just get too drained from the whole process to even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just my face. I mean, I worry about normal things like work deadlines or, you know, paying bills, but nothing like the way I obsess over my face. That's the only thing that gets me stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face led to you avoiding any specific social interactions or work-related activities because of how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. Um, at work, I've actually joined some of my video calls late because I just couldn't get past how I looked on the camera preview. I've even missed a few ticket deadlines because I was so distracted by it. My supervisor actually brought it up in my last performance review, which was really embarrassing. \n\nAnd outside of work, yeah, I've skipped some salsa nights and other community events. Especially if I know people are going to be taking photos. I just can't deal with the anxiety of seeing myself in them later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to enjoy things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Like I mentioned, it's getting in the way of my job. I've missed those ticket deadlines and been late to video calls because I'm just stuck staring at how I look on the screen. It even came up in my performance review with my supervisor, which was tough to hear. \n\nAnd as far as things I enjoy... yeah. I used to love going to salsa nights and different community events, but I've been skipping them. If I know there's a chance people will be taking pictures, I just don't go. It's just not worth the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals to try and make yourself feel better about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I have a meeting or if I'm trying to go out, I get stuck in this loop. I'll open the camera preview on my phone or computer and just keep adjusting the angle and the lighting. I try to fix my hair so it covers that side of my face better. \n\nI end up taking picture after picture, just trying to see if it looks okay, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I do it over and over until I end up being late, or sometimes I just get so drained from the whole process that I don't even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say something *bad* would happen, like a disaster or anything. It's more that... I just couldn't handle it. If I don't check, the anxiety just builds up until I can't focus on anything else. I feel like everyone is staring at that side of my face and judging me. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, I kind of just said this, but no, not terribly wrong like an emergency. It's really just about the anxiety. If I don't check it or try to fix it, I just feel so exposed and panicked that I can't function. That's the only thing that goes wrong, just my own reaction to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would actually look different to others if you didn't perform these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I don't think it physically changes. I mean, I know that just looking at my phone screen or checking a mirror doesn't actually alter my face. \n\nIt's more that... if I don't check, I just don't know exactly how bad it looks in that specific lighting or angle. I guess adjusting my hair changes how much of it people can see, so maybe that part looks different to them. But the actual left side of my face? No, I know that stays the same whether I perform those checks or not. I just... I need to see it so I know what I'm dealing with before I let anyone else see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about your face would decrease if you couldn't check or fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually been in a situation where I couldn't check it at all for a long period of time. But... based on how I feel when I just try to delay checking, I don't think my anxiety would decrease. I think it would probably just stay really high, because I'd just be sitting there obsessing over how bad it might look.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way or be perfectly symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. I feel like it needs to be even. Honestly, that's why I finally came in to see someone. I just... I can't stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks. \n\nIt stands out so much to me on video calls for work, or when I see photos of myself from salsa dancing. I just want it to look symmetrical so I can feel comfortable. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything, but I can't stop seeing it, and it's really starting to mess with my job and my social life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something inherently 'wrong' or 'unacceptable' about the left side of your face, or is it more about fearing how others might react to it?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's a little bit of both. I also definitely worry about how other people are reacting to it. Even though my friends or coworkers say they don't notice anything, I'm always afraid they're just being polite and that they actually think it looks strange. I'm not really sure if I would call it a significant decrease. I mean... it definitely goes down a little, just because I don't have that immediate stress of logging onto a video call for work or running into someone. But the baseline anxiety is still there.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because... I just can't stop focusing on my face. Specifically, the left side of it. It looks really uneven and puffy to me. \n\nI have to be on video calls a lot for work, and I see it there constantly, and in photos from my salsa classes. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything at all when I point it out, but... I do. It's getting to the point where it's really starting to mess with my work and my social life, so I figured I needed to come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of how you feel about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, especially before my work meetings or if I'm supposed to go out somewhere. I just keep opening the camera preview on my screen or my phone. \n\nI'll sit there changing the angle, adjusting the lighting, and... just fixing my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it or make it look normal. I end up retaking pictures over and over to check it, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I just get stuck doing it until I'm either running late, or... honestly, sometimes I just get too drained from the whole process to even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just my face. I mean, I worry about normal things like work deadlines or, you know, paying bills, but nothing like the way I obsess over my face. That's the only thing that gets me stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face led to you avoiding any specific social interactions or work-related activities because of how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. Um, at work, I've actually joined some of my video calls late because I just couldn't get past how I looked on the camera preview. I've even missed a few ticket deadlines because I was so distracted by it. My supervisor actually brought it up in my last performance review, which was really embarrassing. \n\nAnd outside of work, yeah, I've skipped some salsa nights and other community events. Especially if I know people are going to be taking photos. I just can't deal with the anxiety of seeing myself in them later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to enjoy things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Like I mentioned, it's getting in the way of my job. I've missed those ticket deadlines and been late to video calls because I'm just stuck staring at how I look on the screen. It even came up in my performance review with my supervisor, which was tough to hear. \n\nAnd as far as things I enjoy... yeah. I used to love going to salsa nights and different community events, but I've been skipping them. If I know there's a chance people will be taking pictures, I just don't go. It's just not worth the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals to try and make yourself feel better about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I have a meeting or if I'm trying to go out, I get stuck in this loop. I'll open the camera preview on my phone or computer and just keep adjusting the angle and the lighting. I try to fix my hair so it covers that side of my face better. \n\nI end up taking picture after picture, just trying to see if it looks okay, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I do it over and over until I end up being late, or sometimes I just get so drained from the whole process that I don't even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say something *bad* would happen, like a disaster or anything. It's more that... I just couldn't handle it. If I don't check, the anxiety just builds up until I can't focus on anything else. I feel like everyone is staring at that side of my face and judging me. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, I kind of just said this, but no, not terribly wrong like an emergency. It's really just about the anxiety. If I don't check it or try to fix it, I just feel so exposed and panicked that I can't function. That's the only thing that goes wrong, just my own reaction to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would actually look different to others if you didn't perform these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I don't think it physically changes. I mean, I know that just looking at my phone screen or checking a mirror doesn't actually alter my face. \n\nIt's more that... if I don't check, I just don't know exactly how bad it looks in that specific lighting or angle. I guess adjusting my hair changes how much of it people can see, so maybe that part looks different to them. But the actual left side of my face? No, I know that stays the same whether I perform those checks or not. I just... I need to see it so I know what I'm dealing with before I let anyone else see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about your face would decrease if you couldn't check or fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually been in a situation where I couldn't check it at all for a long period of time. But... based on how I feel when I just try to delay checking, I don't think my anxiety would decrease. I think it would probably just stay really high, because I'd just be sitting there obsessing over how bad it might look.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way or be perfectly symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. I feel like it needs to be even. Honestly, that's why I finally came in to see someone. I just... I can't stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks. \n\nIt stands out so much to me on video calls for work, or when I see photos of myself from salsa dancing. I just want it to look symmetrical so I can feel comfortable. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything, but I can't stop seeing it, and it's really starting to mess with my job and my social life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something inherently 'wrong' or 'unacceptable' about the left side of your face, or is it more about fearing how others might react to it?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's a little bit of both. To me, it just looks inherently wrong. I mean, it doesn't match the other side, so it feels unacceptable to me personally when I look at it. \n\nBut... I also definitely worry about how other people are reacting to it. Even though my friends or coworkers say they don't notice anything, I'm always afraid they're just being polite and that they actually think it looks strange. So I guess I feel like it's wrong, and because of that, I'm afraid of what they're thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive behaviors are more about achieving a certain look or preventing something from happening because of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly about trying to achieve a look where it doesn't seem so uneven. Like, before my work meetings or if I'm going out, I'll just keep opening my camera preview. I try changing the angle, adjusting the lighting... I'll fix my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it. \n\nI end up retaking pictures over and over, and asking my friends if I look strange. I just keep doing it until I'm either late or honestly just too drained to even go out. So I guess I'm just trying to fix how it looks before people see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and fix your face if you were completely alone and nobody was going to see you?\n\nPatient: I don't know, I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... I'm not totally sure. Most of the time when I'm checking it, it's because I'm getting ready for a meeting or getting ready to go out. \n\nIf I knew for a fact that no one was ever going to see me... maybe I wouldn't do it as much? But honestly, even when I'm just by myself, I still see it in the mirror and it bothers me. So I might still check it, just because it feels wrong to me. But I'm really not sure how much I'd do it if I was completely alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if nobody was around to judge you?\n\nPatient: I think... I would still feel some anxiety, yes. Even if I'm just alone in my apartment, if I catch a glimpse of myself in the bathroom mirror, it still makes me feel bad. It just looks off to me. So, the anxiety wouldn't completely go away, because I'm still the one seeing it and feeling like it's wrong. But maybe the intense panic about being judged wouldn't be there as much.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about how you feel when you look at it yourself, or is it more about what you think others might think when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to separate the two, honestly. I mean... it's probably a mix of both. I definitely hate how it looks when I see it myself, because it just bothers me on a personal level. But... the really intense stress, the part that makes me keep checking my camera before a work meeting, is mostly about what other people are going to think. I don't really know which one is the bigger issue, to be honest. They just kind of feed into each other.\n\nDoctor: When you're alone and not expecting to see anyone, does your anxiety about your face decrease significantly?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I would call it a significant decrease. I mean... it definitely goes down a little, just because I don't have that immediate stress of logging onto a video call for work or running into someone. But the baseline anxiety is still there. \n\nI haven't really paid attention to exactly how much it drops, to be honest. It just kind of shifts from panicking about other people to just... being bothered by it on my own. So I don't know if the overall anxiety is actually that much lower, or just different.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean... it's probably a mix of both. I definitely hate how it looks when I see it myself, because it just bothers me on a personal level. I don't really know which one is the bigger issue, to be honest. They just kind of feed into each other.\n\nDoctor: When you're alone and not expecting to see anyone, does your anxiety about your face decrease significantly?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I would call it a significant decrease. I mean... it definitely goes down a little, just because I don't have that immediate stress of logging onto a video call for work or running into someone. But the baseline anxiety is still there. \n\nI haven't really paid attention to exactly how much it drops, to be honest. It just kind of shifts from panicking about other people to just... being bothered by it on my own. So I don't know if the overall anxiety is actually that much lower, or just different.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because... I just can't stop focusing on my face. Specifically, the left side of it. It looks really uneven and puffy to me. \n\nI have to be on video calls a lot for work, and I see it there constantly, and in photos from my salsa classes. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything at all when I point it out, but... I do. It's getting to the point where it's really starting to mess with my work and my social life, so I figured I needed to come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of how you feel about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, especially before my work meetings or if I'm supposed to go out somewhere. I just keep opening the camera preview on my screen or my phone. \n\nI'll sit there changing the angle, adjusting the lighting, and... just fixing my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it or make it look normal. I end up retaking pictures over and over to check it, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I just get stuck doing it until I'm either running late, or... honestly, sometimes I just get too drained from the whole process to even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just my face. I mean, I worry about normal things like work deadlines or, you know, paying bills, but nothing like the way I obsess over my face. That's the only thing that gets me stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face led to you avoiding any specific social interactions or work-related activities because of how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. Um, at work, I've actually joined some of my video calls late because I just couldn't get past how I looked on the camera preview. I've even missed a few ticket deadlines because I was so distracted by it. My supervisor actually brought it up in my last performance review, which was really embarrassing. \n\nAnd outside of work, yeah, I've skipped some salsa nights and other community events. Especially if I know people are going to be taking photos. I just can't deal with the anxiety of seeing myself in them later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to enjoy things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Like I mentioned, it's getting in the way of my job. I've missed those ticket deadlines and been late to video calls because I'm just stuck staring at how I look on the screen. It even came up in my performance review with my supervisor, which was tough to hear. \n\nAnd as far as things I enjoy... yeah. I used to love going to salsa nights and different community events, but I've been skipping them. If I know there's a chance people will be taking pictures, I just don't go. It's just not worth the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals to try and make yourself feel better about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I have a meeting or if I'm trying to go out, I get stuck in this loop. I'll open the camera preview on my phone or computer and just keep adjusting the angle and the lighting. I try to fix my hair so it covers that side of my face better. \n\nI end up taking picture after picture, just trying to see if it looks okay, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I do it over and over until I end up being late, or sometimes I just get so drained from the whole process that I don't even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say something *bad* would happen, like a disaster or anything. It's more that... I just couldn't handle it. If I don't check, the anxiety just builds up until I can't focus on anything else. I feel like everyone is staring at that side of my face and judging me. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, I kind of just said this, but no, not terribly wrong like an emergency. It's really just about the anxiety. If I don't check it or try to fix it, I just feel so exposed and panicked that I can't function. That's the only thing that goes wrong, just my own reaction to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would actually look different to others if you didn't perform these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I don't think it physically changes. I mean, I know that just looking at my phone screen or checking a mirror doesn't actually alter my face. \n\nIt's more that... if I don't check, I just don't know exactly how bad it looks in that specific lighting or angle. I guess adjusting my hair changes how much of it people can see, so maybe that part looks different to them. But the actual left side of my face? No, I know that stays the same whether I perform those checks or not. I just... I need to see it so I know what I'm dealing with before I let anyone else see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about your face would decrease if you couldn't check or fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually been in a situation where I couldn't check it at all for a long period of time. But... based on how I feel when I just try to delay checking, I don't think my anxiety would decrease. I think it would probably just stay really high, because I'd just be sitting there obsessing over how bad it might look.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way or be perfectly symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. I feel like it needs to be even. Honestly, that's why I finally came in to see someone. I just... I can't stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks. \n\nIt stands out so much to me on video calls for work, or when I see photos of myself from salsa dancing. I just want it to look symmetrical so I can feel comfortable. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything, but I can't stop seeing it, and it's really starting to mess with my job and my social life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something inherently 'wrong' or 'unacceptable' about the left side of your face, or is it more about fearing how others might react to it?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's a little bit of both. To me, it just looks inherently wrong. I mean, it doesn't match the other side, so it feels unacceptable to me personally when I look at it. \n\nBut... I also definitely worry about how other people are reacting to it. Even though my friends or coworkers say they don't notice anything, I'm always afraid they're just being polite and that they actually think it looks strange. So I guess I feel like it's wrong, and because of that, I'm afraid of what they're thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive behaviors are more about achieving a certain look or preventing something from happening because of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly about trying to achieve a look where it doesn't seem so uneven. Like, before my work meetings or if I'm going out, I'll just keep opening my camera preview. I try changing the angle, adjusting the lighting... I'll fix my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it. \n\nI end up retaking pictures over and over, and asking my friends if I look strange. I just keep doing it until I'm either late or honestly just too drained to even go out. So I guess I'm just trying to fix how it looks before people see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and fix your face if you were completely alone and nobody was going to see you?\n\nPatient: I don't know, I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... I'm not totally sure. Most of the time when I'm checking it, it's because I'm getting ready for a meeting or getting ready to go out. \n\nIf I knew for a fact that no one was ever going to see me... maybe I wouldn't do it as much? But honestly, even when I'm just by myself, I still see it in the mirror and it bothers me. So I might still check it, just because it feels wrong to me. But I'm really not sure how much I'd do it if I was completely alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if nobody was around to judge you?\n\nPatient: I think... I would still feel some anxiety, yes. Even if I'm just alone in my apartment, if I catch a glimpse of myself in the bathroom mirror, it still makes me feel bad. It just looks off to me. So, the anxiety wouldn't completely go away, because I'm still the one seeing it and feeling like it's wrong. But maybe the intense panic about being judged wouldn't be there as much.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about how you feel when you look at it yourself, or is it more about what you think others might think when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to separate the two, honestly. I mean... it's probably a mix of both. I definitely hate how it looks when I see it myself, because it just bothers me on a personal level. But... the really intense stress, the part that makes me keep checking my camera before a work meeting, is mostly about what other people are going to think. I don't really know which one is the bigger issue, to be honest. They just kind of feed into each other.\n\nDoctor: When you're alone and not expecting to see anyone, does your anxiety about your face decrease significantly?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I would call it a significant decrease. It just kind of shifts from panicking about other people to just... being bothered by it on my own. So I don't know if the overall anxiety is actually that much lower, or just different.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling anxious about your face, is it more because of how you think it looks to you or because of what you think others might be thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I think we just talked about that... Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything, but I can't stop seeing it, and it's really starting to mess with my job and my social life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something inherently 'wrong' or 'unacceptable' about the left side of your face, or is it more about fearing how others might react to it?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's a little bit of both. To me, it just looks inherently wrong. I mean, it doesn't match the other side, so it feels unacceptable to me personally when I look at it. \n\nBut... I also definitely worry about how other people are reacting to it. Even though my friends or coworkers say they don't notice anything, I'm always afraid they're just being polite and that they actually think it looks strange. So I guess I feel like it's wrong, and because of that, I'm afraid of what they're thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive behaviors are more about achieving a certain look or preventing something from happening because of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly about trying to achieve a look where it doesn't seem so uneven. Like, before my work meetings or if I'm going out, I'll just keep opening my camera preview. I try changing the angle, adjusting the lighting... I'll fix my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it. \n\nI end up retaking pictures over and over, and asking my friends if I look strange. I just keep doing it until I'm either late or honestly just too drained to even go out. So I guess I'm just trying to fix how it looks before people see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and fix your face if you were completely alone and nobody was going to see you?\n\nPatient: I don't know, I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... I'm not totally sure. Most of the time when I'm checking it, it's because I'm getting ready for a meeting or getting ready to go out. \n\nIf I knew for a fact that no one was ever going to see me... maybe I wouldn't do it as much? But honestly, even when I'm just by myself, I still see it in the mirror and it bothers me. So I might still check it, just because it feels wrong to me. But I'm really not sure how much I'd do it if I was completely alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if nobody was around to judge you?\n\nPatient: I think... I would still feel some anxiety, yes. Even if I'm just alone in my apartment, if I catch a glimpse of myself in the bathroom mirror, it still makes me feel bad. It just looks off to me. So, the anxiety wouldn't completely go away, because I'm still the one seeing it and feeling like it's wrong. But maybe the intense panic about being judged wouldn't be there as much.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about how you feel when you look at it yourself, or is it more about what you think others might think when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to separate the two, honestly. I mean... it's probably a mix of both. I definitely hate how it looks when I see it myself, because it just bothers me on a personal level. But... the really intense stress, the part that makes me keep checking my camera before a work meeting, is mostly about what other people are going to think. I don't really know which one is the bigger issue, to be honest. They just kind of feed into each other.\n\nDoctor: When you're alone and not expecting to see anyone, does your anxiety about your face decrease significantly?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I would call it a significant decrease. I mean... it definitely goes down a little, just because I don't have that immediate stress of logging onto a video call for work or running into someone. But the baseline anxiety is still there. \n\nI haven't really paid attention to exactly how much it drops, to be honest. It just kind of shifts from panicking about other people to just... being bothered by it on my own. So I don't know if the overall anxiety is actually that much lower, or just different.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling anxious about your face, is it more because of how you think it looks to you or because of what you think others might be thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I think we just talked about that... I'm really not sure how to separate the two. It's a mix. I hate how it looks to me, but the really bad stress is about what other people think. I don't know which one causes more anxiety overall.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's just my face. I mean, I worry about normal things like work deadlines or, you know, paying bills, but nothing like the way I obsess over my face. That's the only thing that gets me stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face led to you avoiding any specific social interactions or work-related activities because of how you feel about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. Um, at work, I've actually joined some of my video calls late because I just couldn't get past how I looked on the camera preview. I've even missed a few ticket deadlines because I was so distracted by it. My supervisor actually brought it up in my last performance review, which was really embarrassing. \n\nAnd outside of work, yeah, I've skipped some salsa nights and other community events. Especially if I know people are going to be taking photos. I just can't deal with the anxiety of seeing myself in them later.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to enjoy things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Like I mentioned, it's getting in the way of my job. I've missed those ticket deadlines and been late to video calls because I'm just stuck staring at how I look on the screen. It even came up in my performance review with my supervisor, which was tough to hear. \n\nAnd as far as things I enjoy... yeah. I used to love going to salsa nights and different community events, but I've been skipping them. If I know there's a chance people will be taking pictures, I just don't go. It's just not worth the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals to try and make yourself feel better about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I have a meeting or if I'm trying to go out, I get stuck in this loop. I'll open the camera preview on my phone or computer and just keep adjusting the angle and the lighting. I try to fix my hair so it covers that side of my face better. \n\nI end up taking picture after picture, just trying to see if it looks okay, and I'll keep asking my friends if I look strange. I do it over and over until I end up being late, or sometimes I just get so drained from the whole process that I don't even go out at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face or try to fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say something *bad* would happen, like a disaster or anything. It's more that... I just couldn't handle it. If I don't check, the anxiety just builds up until I can't focus on anything else. I feel like everyone is staring at that side of my face and judging me. So I guess the \"bad\" thing is just how overwhelming the panic gets if I don't try to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't perform these rituals, something would go terribly wrong?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, I kind of just said this, but no, not terribly wrong like an emergency. It's really just about the anxiety. If I don't check it or try to fix it, I just feel so exposed and panicked that I can't function. That's the only thing that goes wrong, just my own reaction to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would actually look different to others if you didn't perform these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I don't think it physically changes. I mean, I know that just looking at my phone screen or checking a mirror doesn't actually alter my face. \n\nIt's more that... if I don't check, I just don't know exactly how bad it looks in that specific lighting or angle. I guess adjusting my hair changes how much of it people can see, so maybe that part looks different to them. But the actual left side of my face? No, I know that stays the same whether I perform those checks or not. I just... I need to see it so I know what I'm dealing with before I let anyone else see me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about your face would decrease if you couldn't check or fix how it looks?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually been in a situation where I couldn't check it at all for a long period of time. But... based on how I feel when I just try to delay checking, I don't think my anxiety would decrease. I think it would probably just stay really high, because I'd just be sitting there obsessing over how bad it might look.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look a certain way or be perfectly symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. I feel like it needs to be even. Honestly, that's why I finally came in to see someone. I just... I can't stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks. \n\nIt stands out so much to me on video calls for work, or when I see photos of myself from salsa dancing. I just want it to look symmetrical so I can feel comfortable. Everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything, but I can't stop seeing it, and it's really starting to mess with my job and my social life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something inherently 'wrong' or 'unacceptable' about the left side of your face, or is it more about fearing how others might react to it?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's a little bit of both. To me, it just looks inherently wrong. I mean, it doesn't match the other side, so it feels unacceptable to me personally when I look at it. \n\nBut... I also definitely worry about how other people are reacting to it. Even though my friends or coworkers say they don't notice anything, I'm always afraid they're just being polite and that they actually think it looks strange. So I guess I feel like it's wrong, and because of that, I'm afraid of what they're thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive behaviors are more about achieving a certain look or preventing something from happening because of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly about trying to achieve a look where it doesn't seem so uneven. Like, before my work meetings or if I'm going out, I'll just keep opening my camera preview. I try changing the angle, adjusting the lighting... I'll fix my hair around that side of my face to see if I can hide it. \n\nI end up retaking pictures over and over, and asking my friends if I look strange. I just keep doing it until I'm either late or honestly just too drained to even go out. I guess... I'm not totally sure. Most of the time when I'm checking it, it's because I'm getting ready for a meeting or getting ready to go out. \n\nIf I knew for a fact that no one was ever going to see me... maybe I wouldn't do it as much? But honestly, even when I'm just by myself, I still see it in the mirror and it bothers me. So I might still check it, just because it feels wrong to me. But I'm really not sure how much I'd do it if I was completely alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if nobody was around to judge you?\n\nPatient: I think... I would still feel some anxiety, yes. Even if I'm just alone in my apartment, if I catch a glimpse of myself in the bathroom mirror, it still makes me feel bad. It just looks off to me. So, the anxiety wouldn't completely go away, because I'm still the one seeing it and feeling like it's wrong. But maybe the intense panic about being judged wouldn't be there as much.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about how you feel when you look at it yourself, or is it more about what you think others might think when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to separate the two, honestly. I mean... it's probably a mix of both. I definitely hate how it looks when I see it myself, because it just bothers me on a personal level. But... the really intense stress, the part that makes me keep checking my camera before a work meeting, is mostly about what other people are going to think. I don't really know which one is the bigger issue, to be honest. They just kind of feed into each other.\n\nDoctor: When you're alone and not expecting to see anyone, does your anxiety about your face decrease significantly?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I would call it a significant decrease. I mean... it definitely goes down a little, just because I don't have that immediate stress of logging onto a video call for work or running into someone. But the baseline anxiety is still there. \n\nI haven't really paid attention to exactly how much it drops, to be honest. It just kind of shifts from panicking about other people to just... being bothered by it on my own. So I don't know if the overall anxiety is actually that much lower, or just different.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling anxious about your face, is it more because of how you think it looks to you or because of what you think others might be thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I think we just talked about that... I'm really not sure how to separate the two. It's a mix. I hate how it looks to me, but the really bad stress is about what other people think. I don't know which one causes more anxiety overall.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling anxious about your face, is it more because you dislike how it looks to you or because you worry about what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like you're asking me the exact same question again. Like I said just a moment ago, it's really both. I can't untangle them. I dislike it myself, and I worry about others seeing it. I don't have a better way to explain it or pick one over the other."
